i < len(keptQueues); i++ {\n\t\tkeptQueues[i].index--\n\t}\n\treturn keptQueues\n}\n\nfunc (qs *queueSet) Dump(includeRequestDetails bool) debug.QueueSetDump {\n\tqs.lock.Lock()\n\tdefer qs.lock.Unlock()\n\td := debug.QueueSetDump{\n\t\tQueues:                     make([]debug.QueueDump, len(qs.queues)),\n\t\tQueuelessExecutingRequests: SetMapReduce(dumpRequest(includeRequestDetails), append1[debug.RequestDump])(qs.requestsExecutingSet),\n\t\tWaiting:                    qs.totRequestsWaiting,\n\t\tExecuting:                  qs.totRequestsExecuting,\n\t\tSeatsInUse:                 qs.totSeatsInUse,\n\t\tSeatsWaiting:               qs.totSeatsWaiting,\n\t\tDispatched:                 qs.totRequestsDispatched,\n\t\tRejected:                   qs.totRequestsRejected,\n\t\tTimedout:                   qs.totRequestsTimedout,\n\t\tCancelled:                  qs.totRequestsCancelled,\n\t}\n\tfor i, q := range qs.queues {\n\t\td.Queues[i] = q.dumpLocked(includeRequestDetails)\n\t}\n\treturn d\n}\n\nfunc OnRequestDispatched(r fq.Request) {\n\treq, ok := r.(*request)\n\tif !ok {\n\t\treturn\n\t}\n\n\tqs := req.qs\n\tif qs != nil {\n\t\tqs.lock.Lock()\n\t\tdefer qs.lock.Unlock()\n\t\tqs.totRequestsDispatched++\n\t}\n}\n","numLines":1030,"startLine":1,"totalLines":1030}}}

It covers the\n// functionality described in the \"Assignment to a Queue\", \"Queuing\",\n// and \"Dispatching\" sections of\n// https://github.com/kubernetes/enhancements/blob/master/keps/sig-api-machinery/1040-priority-and-fairness/README.md\n// .  Some day we may have connections between priority levels, but\n// today is not that day.\ntype QueueSet interface {\n\t// BeginConfigChange starts the two-step process of updating the\n\t// configuration.  No change is made until Complete is called.  If\n\t// `C := X.BeginConstruction(q)` then `C.Complete(d)` returns the\n\t// same value `X`.  If the QueuingConfig's DesiredNumQueues field\n\t// is zero then the other queuing-specific config parameters are\n\t// not changed, so that the queues continue draining as before.\n\t// In any case, reconfiguration does not discard any queue unless\n\t// and until it is undesired and empty.\n\tBeginConfigChange(QueuingConfig) (QueueSetCompleter, error)\n\n\t// IsIdle returns a bool indicating whether the QueueSet was idle\n\t// at the moment of the return.  Idle means the QueueSet has zero\n\t// requests queued and zero executing.  This bit can change only\n\t// (1) during a call to StartRequest and (2) during a call to\n\t// Request::Finish.  In the latter case idleness can only change\n\t// from false to true.\n\tIsIdle() bool\n\n\t// StartRequest begins the process of handling a request.  If the\n\t// request gets queued and the number of queues is greater than 1\n\t// then StartRequest uses the given hashValue as the source of\n\t// entropy as it shuffle-shards the request into a queue.  The\n\t// descr1 and descr2 values play no role in the logic but appear\n\t// in log messages.  This method always returns quickly (without\n\t// waiting for the request to be dequeued). given demands (which slice is not side-effected).\n    52→func fairAlloc(demands []float64, capacity float64) []float64 {\n    53→\tcount := len(demands)\n    54→\tindices := make([]int, count)\n    55→\tfor i := 0; i < count; i++ {\n    56→\t\tindices[i] = i\n    57→\t}\n    58→\tsort.Slice(indices, func(i, j int) bool { return demands[indices[i]] < demands[indices[j]] })\n    59→\talloc := make([]float64, count)\n    60→\tvar next int\n    61→\tvar prevAlloc float64\n    62→\tfor ; next < count; next++ {\n    63→\t\t// `capacity` is how much remains assuming that\n    64→\t\t// all unvisited items get `prevAlloc`.\n    65→\t\tidx := indices[next]\n    66→\t\tdemand := demands[idx]\n    67→\t\tif demand <= 0 {\n    68→\t\t\tcontinue\n    69→\t\t}\n    70→\t\t// `fullCapacityBite` is how much more capacity would be used\n    71→\t\t// if this and all following items get as much as this one\n    72→\t\t// is demanding.\n    73→\t\tfullCapacityBite := float64(count-next) * (demand - prevAlloc)\n    74→\t\tif fullCapacityBite > capacity {\n    75→\t\t\tbreak\n    76→\t\t}\n    77→\t\tprevAlloc = demand\n    78→\t\talloc[idx] = demand\n    79→\t\tcapacity -= fullCapacityBite\n    80→\t}\n    81→\tfor j := next; j < count; j++ {\n    82→\t\talloc[indices[j]] = prevAlloc + capacity/float64(count-next)\n    83→\t}\n    84→\treturn alloc\n    85→}\n    86→\n side-effected).\nfunc fairAlloc(demands []float64, capacity float64) []float64 {\n\tcount := len(demands)\n\tindices := make([]int, count)\n\tfor i := 0; i < count; i++ {\n\t\tindices[i] = i\n\t}\n\tsort.Slice(indices, func(i, j int) bool { return demands[indices[i]] < demands[indices[j]] })\n\talloc := make([]float64, count)\n\tvar next int\n\tvar prevAlloc float64\n\tfor ; next < count; next++ {\n\t\t// `capacity` is how much remains assuming that\n\t\t// all unvisited items get `prevAlloc`.\n\t\tidx := indices[next]\n\t\tdemand := demands[idx]\n\t\tif demand <= 0 := float64(count-next) * (demand - prevAlloc)\n\t\tif fullCapacityBite > capacity {\n\t\t\tbreak\n\t\t}\n\t\tprevAlloc = demand\n\t\talloc[idx] = demand\n\t\tcapacity -= fullCapacityBite\n\t}\n\tfor j := next; j < count; j++ {\n\t\talloc[indices[j]] = prevAlloc + capacity/float64(count-next)\n\t}\n\treturn alloc\n}\n\nfunc TestFairAlloc(t *testing.T) {\n\tif e, a := []float64{0, 0}, {"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01CTHvrpzkx6f4YpmP8a26aA","type":"tool_result","content":"{\"content\":\"1: /*\\n2: Copyright 2019 The Kubernetes Authors.\\n3: \\n4: Licensed under the Apache License, Version 2.0 (the \\\"License\\\");\\n5: you may not use this file except in compliance with the License.\\n6: You may obtain a copy of the License at\\n7: \\n8:     http://www.apache.org/licenses/LICENSE-2.0\\n9: \\n10: Unless required by applicable law or agreed to in writing, software\\n11: distributed under the License is distributed on an \\\"AS IS\\\" BASIS,\\n12: WITHOUT WARRANTIES OR CONDITIONS OF ANY KIND, either express or implied.\\n13: See the License for the specific language governing permissions and\\n14: limitations under the License.\\n15: */\\n16: \\n17: // Package queueset implements a technique called \\\"fair queuing for\\n18: // server requests\\\".  One QueueSet is a set of queues operating\\n19: // according to this technique.\\n20: //\\n21: // Fair queuing for server requests is inspired by the fair queuing\\n22: // technique from the world of networking.  You can find a good paper\\n23: // on that at https://dl.acm.org/citation.cfm?doid=75247.75248 or\\n24: // http://people.csail.mit.edu/imcgraw/links/research/pubs/networks/WFQ.pdf\\n25: // and there is an implementation outline in the Wikipedia article at\\n26: // https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fair_queuing .\\n27: //\\n28: // Fair queuing for server requests differs from traditional fair\\n29: // queuing in three ways: (1) we are dispatching application layer\\n30: // requests to a server rather than transmitting packets on a network\\n31: // link, (2) multiple requests can be executing at once, and (3) the\\n32: // service time (execution duration) is not known until the execution\\n33: // completes.\\n34: //\\n35: // The first two differences can easily be handled by straightforward\\n36: // adaptation of the concept called \\\"R(t)\\\" in the original paper and\\n37: // \\\"virtual time\\\" in the implementation outline. In that\\n38: // implementation outline, the notation now() is used to mean reading\\n39: // the virtual clock. In the original paper’s terms, \\\"R(t)\\\" is the\\n40: // number of \\\"rounds\\\" that have been completed at real time t ---\\n41: // where a round consists of virtually transmitting one bit from every\\n42: // non-empty queue in the router (regardless of which queue holds the\\n43: // packet that is really being transmitted at the moment); in this\\n44: // conception, a packet is considered to be \\\"in\\\" its queue until the\\n45: // packet’s transmission is finished. For our problem, we can define a\\n46: // round to be giving one nanosecond of CPU to every non-empty queue\\n47: // in the apiserver (where emptiness is judged based on both queued\\n48: // and executing requests from that queue), and define R(t) = (server\\n49: // start time) + (1 ns) * (number of rounds since server start). Let\\n50: // us write NEQ(t) for that number of non-empty queues in the\\n51: // apiserver at time t.  Let us also write C for the concurrency\\n52: // limit.  In the original paper, the partial derivative of R(t) with\\n53: // respect to t is\\n54: //\\n55: //\\t1 / NEQ(t) .\\n56: //\\n57: // To generalize from transmitting one packet at a time to executing C\\n58: // requests at a time, that derivative becomes\\n59: //\\n60: //\\tC / NEQ(t) .\\n61: //\\n62: // However, sometimes there are fewer than C requests available to\\n63: // execute.  For a given queue \\\"q\\\", let us also write \\\"reqs(q, t)\\\" for\\n64: // the number of requests of that queue that are executing at that\\n65: // time.  The total number of requests executing is sum[over q]\\n66: // reqs(q, t) and if that is less than C then virtual time is not\\n67: // advancing as fast as it would if all C seats were occupied; in this\\n68: // case the numerator of the quotient in that derivative should be\\n69: // adjusted proportionally.  Putting it all together for fair queing\\n70: // for server requests: at a particular time t, the partial derivative\\n71: // of R(t) with respect to t is\\n72: //\\n73: //\\tmin( C, sum[over q] reqs(q, t) ) / NEQ(t) .\\n74: //\\n75: // In terms of the implementation outline, this is the rate at which\\n76: // virtual time is advancing at time t (in virtual nanoseconds per\\n77: // real nanosecond). Where the networking implementation outline adds\\n78: // packet size to a virtual time, in our version this corresponds to\\n79: // adding a service time (i.e., duration) to virtual time.\\n80: //\\n81: // The third difference is handled by modifying the algorithm to\\n82: // dispatch based on an initial guess at the request’s service time\\n83: // (duration) and then make the corresponding adjustments once the\\n84: // request’s actual service time is known. This is similar, although\\n85: // not exactly isomorphic, to the original paper’s adjustment by\\n86: // `$\\\\delta$` for the sake of promptness.\\n87: //\\n88: // For implementation simplicity (see below), let us use the same\\n89: // initial service time guess for every request; call that duration\\n90: // G. A good choice might be the service time limit (1\\n91: // minute). Different guesses will give slightly different dynamics,\\n92: // but any positive number can be used for G without ruining the\\n93: // long-term behavior.\\n94: //\\n95: // As in ordinary fair queuing, there is a bound on divergence from\\n96: // the ideal. In plain fair queuing the bound is one packet; in our\\n97: // version it is C requests.\\n98: //\\n99: // To support efficiently making the necessary adjustments once a\\n100: // request’s actual service time is known, the virtual finish time of\\n101: // a request and the last virtual finish time of a queue are not\\n102: // represented directly but instead computed from queue length,\\n103: // request position in the queue, and an alternate state variable that\\n104: // holds the queue’s virtual start time. While the queue is empty and\\n105: // has no requests executing: the value of its virtual start time\\n106: // variable is ignored and its last virtual finish time is considered\\n107: // to be in the virtual past. When a request arrives to an empty queue\\n108: // with no requests executing, the queue’s virtual start time is set\\n109: // to the current virtual time. The virtual finish time of request\\n110: // number J in the queue (counting from J=1 for the head) is J * G +\\n111: // (queue's virtual start time). While the queue is non-empty: the\\n112: // last virtual finish time of the queue is the virtual finish time of\\n113: // the last request in the queue. While the queue is empty and has a\\n114: // request executing: the last virtual finish time is the queue’s\\n115: // virtual start time. When a request is dequeued for service the\\n116: // queue’s virtual start time is advanced by G. In that\\n38: // implementation outline, the notation now() is used to mean reading\\n39: // the virtual clock. In the original paper’s terms, \\\"R(t)\\\" is the\\n40: // number of \\\"rounds\\\" that have been completed at real time t ---\\n41: // where a round consists of virtually transmitting one bit from every\\n42: // non-empty queue in the router (regardless of which queue holds the\\n43: // packet that is really being transmitted at the moment); in this\\n44: // conception, a packet is considered to be \\\"in\\\" its queue until the\\n45: // packet’s transmission is finished. For our problem, we can define a\\n46: // round to be giving one nanosecond of CPU to every non-empty queue\\n47: // in the apiserver (where emptiness is judged based on both queued\\n48: // and executing requests from that queue), and define R(t) = (server\\n49: // start time) + (1 ns) * (number of rounds since server start). {"type":"assistant","message":{"model":"claude-opus-4-5-20251101","id":"msg_01AaVB61CssX7MHbvJABs2go","type":"message","role":"assistant","content":[{"type":"tool_use","id":"toolu_01RDxq7SB73BmrKFkueHi1pG","name":"Write","input":{"file_path":"/workspace/staging/src/k8s.io/apiserver/pkg/util/flowcontrol/fairqueuing/queueset/doc.go","content":"/*\nCopyright 2019 The Kubernetes Authors.\n\nLicensed under the Apache License, Version 2.0 (the \"License\");\nyou may not use this file except in compliance with the License.\nYou may obtain a copy of the License at\n\n    http://www.apache.org/licenses/LICENSE-2.0\n\nUnless required by applicable law or agreed to in writing, software\ndistributed under the License is distributed on an \"AS IS\" BASIS,\nWITHOUT WARRANTIES OR CONDITIONS OF ANY KIND, either express or implied.\nSee the License for the specific language governing permissions and\nlimitations under the License.\n*/\n\n// Package queueset implements a technique called \"fair queuing for\n// server requests\".  One QueueSet is a set of queues operating\n// according to this technique.\n//\n// Fair queuing for server requests is inspired by the fair queuing\n// technique from the world of networking.  You can find a good paper\n// on that at https://dl.acm.org/citation.cfm?doid=75247.75248 or\n// http://people.csail.mit.edu/imcgraw/links/research/pubs/networks/WFQ.pdf\n// and there is an implementation outline in the Wikipedia article at\n// https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fair_queuing .\n//\n// Fair queuing for server requests differs from traditional fair\n// queuing in three ways: (1) we are dispatching application layer\n// requests to a server rather than transmitting packets on a network\n// link, (2) multiple requests can be executing at once, and (3) the\n// service time (execution duration) is not known until the execution\n// completes.\n//\n// The first two differences can easily be handled by straightforward\n// adaptation of the concept called \"R(t)\" in the original paper and\n// \"virtual time\" in the implementation outline. In that\n// implementation outline, the notation now() is used to mean reading\n// the virtual clock. In the original paper's terms, \"R(t)\" is the\n// number of \"rounds\" that have been completed at real time t ---\n// where a round consists of virtually transmitting one bit from every\n// non-empty queue in the router (regardless of which queue holds the\n// packet that is really being transmitted at the moment); in this\n// conception, a packet is considered to be \"in\" its queue until the\n// packet's transmission is finished. For our problem, we can define a\n// round to be giving one nanosecond of CPU to every non-empty queue\n// in the apiserver (where emptiness is judged based on both queued\n// and executing requests from that queue), and define R(t) = (server\n// start time) + (1 ns) * (number of rounds since server start). Let\n// us write NEQ(t) for that number of non-empty queues in the\n// apiserver at time t.  Let us also write C for the concurrency\n// limit.  In the original paper, the partial derivative of R(t) with\n// respect to t is\n//\n//\t1 / NEQ(t) .\n//\n// To generalize from transmitting one packet at a time to executing C\n// requests at a time, that derivative becomes\n//\n//\tC / NEQ(t) .\n//\n// However, sometimes there are fewer than C requests available to\n// execute.  For a given queue \"q\", let us also write \"reqs(q, t)\" for\n// the number of requests of that queue that are executing at that\n// time.  The total number of requests executing is sum[over q]\n// reqs(q, t) and if that is less than C then virtual time is not\n// advancing as fast as it would if all C seats were occupied; in this\n// case the numerator of the quotient in that derivative should be\n// adjusted proportionally.  Putting it all together for fair queing\n// for server requests: at a particular time t, the partial derivative\n// of R(t) with respect to t is\n//\n//\tmin( C, sum[over q] reqs(q, t) ) / NEQ(t) .\n//\n// In terms of the implementation outline, this is the rate at which\n// virtual time is advancing at time t (in virtual nanoseconds per\n// real nanosecond). Where the networking implementation outline adds\n// packet size to a virtual time, in our version this corresponds to\n// adding a service time (i.e., duration) to virtual time.\n//\n// The third difference is handled by modifying the algorithm to\n// dispatch based on an initial guess at the request's service time\n// (duration) and then make the corresponding adjustments once the\n// request's actual service time is known. This is similar, although\n// not exactly isomorphic, to the original paper's adjustment by\n// `$\\delta$` for the sake of promptness.\n//\n// For implementation simplicity (see below), let us use the same\n// initial service time guess for every request; call that duration\n// G. A good choice might be the service time limit (1\n// minute). Different guesses will give slightly different dynamics,\n// but any positive number can be used for G without ruining the\n// long-term behavior.\n//\n// As in ordinary fair queuing, there is a bound on divergence from\n// the ideal. In plain fair queuing the bound is one packet; in our\n// version it is C requests.\n//\n// To support efficiently making the necessary adjustments once a\n// request's actual service time is known, the virtual finish time of\n// a request and the last virtual finish time of a queue are not\n// represented directly but instead computed from queue length,\n// request position in the queue, and an alternate state variable that\n// holds the queue's virtual start time. While the queue is empty and\n// has no requests executing: the value of its virtual start time\n// variable is ignored and its last virtual finish time is considered\n// to be in the virtual past. When a request arrives to an empty queue\n// with no requests executing, the queue's virtual start time is set\n// to the current virtual time. The virtual finish time of request\n// number J in the queue (counting from J=1 for the head) is J * G +\n// (queue's virtual start time). While the queue is non-empty: the\n// last virtual finish time of the queue is the virtual finish time of\n// the last request in the queue. While the queue is empty and has a\n// request executing: the last virtual finish time is the queue's\n// virtual start time. When a request is dequeued for service the\n// queue's virtual start time is advanced by G. When a request\n// finishes being served, and the actual service time was S, the\n// queue's virtual start time is decremented by G - S.\npackage queueset\n"}}],"stop_reason":null,"stop_sequence":null,"usage":{"input_tokens":0,"cache_creation_input_tokens":250,"cache_read_input_tokens":69210,"cache_creation":{"ephemeral_5m_input_tokens":250,"ephemeral_1h_input_tokens":0},"output_tokens":1,"service_tier":"standard"},"context_management":null},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"f3b46ffe-ca2a-49ac-8d5b-8a7d7221b87d","uuid":"faa4e320-9957-4f76-bfa3-b2e1a8695632"}
{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01RDxq7SB73BmrKFkueHi1pG","type":"tool_result","content":"File created successfully at: /workspace/staging/src/k8s.io/apiserver/pkg/util/flowcontrol/fairqueuing/queueset/doc.go"}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"f3b46ffe-ca2a-49ac-8d5b-8a7d7221b87d","uuid":"21394516-4e2a-4384-b1da-c0e54bd0529b","tool_use_result":{"type":"create","filePath":"/workspace/staging/src/k8s.io/apiserver/pkg/util/flowcontrol/fairqueuing/queueset/doc.go","content":"/*\nCopyright 2019 The Kubernetes Authors.\n\nLicensed under the Apache License, Version 2.0 (the \"License\");\nyou may not use this file except in compliance with the License.\nYou may obtain a copy of the License at\n\n    http://www.apache.org/licenses/LICENSE-2.0\n\nUnless required by applicable law or agreed to in writing, software\ndistributed under the License is distributed on an \"AS IS\" BASIS,\nWITHOUT WARRANTIES OR CONDITIONS OF ANY KIND, either express or implied.\nSee the License for the specific language governing permissions and\nlimitations under the License.\n*/\n\n// Package queueset implements a technique called \"fair queuing for\n// server requests\".  One QueueSet is a set of queues operating\n// according to this technique.\n//\n// Fair queuing for server requests is inspired by the fair queuing\n// technique from the world of networking.  You can find a good paper\n// on that at https://dl.acm.org/citation.cfm?doid=75247.75248 or\n// http://people.csail.mit.edu/imcgraw/links/research/pubs/networks/WFQ.pdf\n// and there is an implementation outline in the Wikipedia article at\n// https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fair_queuing .\n//\n// Fair queuing for server requests differs from traditional fair\n// queuing in three ways: (1) we are dispatching application layer\n// requests to a server rather than transmitting packets on a network\n// link, (2) multiple requests can be executing at once, and (3) the\n// service time (execution duration) is not known until the execution\n// completes.\n//\n// The first two differences can easily be handled by straightforward\n// adaptation of the concept called \"R(t)\" in the original paper and\n// \"virtual time\" in the implementation outline. In that\n// implementation outline, the notation now() is used to mean reading\n// the virtual clock. In the original paper's terms, \"R(t)\" is the\n// number of \"rounds\" that have been completed at real time t ---\n// where a round consists of virtually transmitting one bit from every\n// non-empty queue in the router (regardless of which queue holds the\n// packet that is really being transmitted at the moment); in this\n// conception, a packet is considered to be \"in\" its queue until the\n// packet's transmission is finished. For our problem, we can define a\n// round to be giving one nanosecond of CPU to every non-empty queue\n// in the apiserver (where emptiness is judged based on both queued\n// and executing requests from that queue), and define R(t) = (server\n// start time) + (1 ns) * (number of rounds since server start). Let\n// us write NEQ(t) for that number of non-empty queues in the\n// apiserver at time t.  Let us also write C for the concurrency\n// limit.  In the original paper, the partial derivative of R(t) with\n// respect to t is\n//\n//\t1 / NEQ(t) .\n//\n// To generalize from transmitting one packet at a time to executing C\n// requests at a time, that derivative becomes\n//\n//\tC / NEQ(t) .\n//\n// However, sometimes there are fewer than C requests available to\n// execute.  For a given queue \"q\", let us also write \"reqs(q, t)\" for\n// the number of requests of that queue that are executing at that\n// time.  The total number of requests executing is sum[over q]\n// reqs(q, t) and if that is less than C then virtual time is not\n// advancing as fast as it would if all C seats were occupied; in this\n// case the numerator of the quotient in that derivative should be\n// adjusted proportionally.  Putting it all together for fair queing\n// for server requests: at a particular time t, the partial derivative\n// of R(t) with respect to t is\n//\n//\tmin( C, sum[over q] reqs(q, t) ) / NEQ(t) .\n//\n// In terms of the implementation outline, this is the rate at which\n// virtual time is advancing at time t (in virtual nanoseconds per\n// real nanosecond). Where the networking implementation outline adds\n// packet size to a virtual time, in our version this corresponds to\n// adding a service time (i.e., duration) to virtual time.\n//\n// The third difference is handled by modifying the algorithm to\n// dispatch based on an initial guess at the request's service time\n// (duration) and then make the corresponding adjustments once the\n// request's actual service time is known. This is similar, although\n// not exactly isomorphic, to the original paper's adjustment by\n// `$\\delta$` for the sake of promptness.\n//\n// For implementation simplicity (see below), let us use the same\n// initial service time guess for every request; call that duration\n// G. A good choice might be the service time limit (1\n// minute). Different guesses will give slightly different dynamics,\n// but any positive number can be used for G without ruining the\n// long-term behavior.\n//\n// As in ordinary fair queuing, there is a bound on divergence from\n// the ideal. In plain fair queuing the bound is one packet; in our\n// version it is C requests.\n//\n// To support efficiently making the necessary adjustments once a\n// request's actual service time is known, the virtual finish time of\n// a request and the last virtual finish time of a queue are not\n// represented directly but instead computed from queue length,\n// request position in the queue, and an alternate state variable that\n// holds the queue's virtual start time. While the queue is empty and\n// has no requests executing: the value of its virtual start time\n// variable is ignored and its last virtual finish time is considered\n// to be in the virtual past. When a request arrives to an empty queue\n// with no requests executing, the queue's virtual start time is set\n// to the current virtual time. The virtual finish time of request\n// number J in the queue (counting from J=1 for the head) is J * G +\n// (queue's virtual start time). While the queue is non-empty: the\n// last virtual finish time of the queue is the virtual finish time of\n// the last request in the queue. While the queue is empty and has a\n// request executing: the last virtual finish time is the queue's\n// virtual start time. When a request is dequeued for service the\n// queue's virtual start time is advanced by G. When a request\n// finishes being served, and the actual service time was S, the\n// queue's virtual start time is decremented by G - S.\npackage queueset\n","structuredPatch":[],"originalFile":null}}

One QueueSet is a set of queues operating\n// according to this technique.\n//\n// Fair queuing for server requests is inspired by the fair queuing\n// technique from the world of networking.  You can find a good paper\n// on that at https://dl.acm.org/citation.cfm?doid=75247.75248 or\n// http://people.csail.mit.edu/imcgraw/links/research/pubs/networks/WFQ.pdf\n// and there is an implementation outline in the Wikipedia article at\n// https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fair_queuing .\n//\n// Fair queuing for server requests differs from traditional fair\n// queuing in three ways: (1) we are dispatching application layer\n// requests to a server rather than transmitting packets on a network\n// link, (2) multiple requests can be executing at once, and (3) the\n// service time (execution duration) is not known until the execution\n// completes.\n//\n// The first two differences can easily be handled by straightforward\n// adaptation of the concept called \"R(t)\" in the original paper and\n// \"virtual time\" in the implementation outline. In that\n// implementation outline, the notation now() is used to mean reading\n// the virtual clock. In the original paper's terms, \"R(t)\" is the\n// number of \"rounds\" that have been completed at real time t ---\n// where a round consists of virtually transmitting one bit from every\n// non-empty queue in the router (regardless of which queue holds the\n// packet that is really being transmitted at the moment); in this\n// conception, a packet is considered to be \"in\" its queue until the\n// packet's transmission is finished. For our problem, we can define a\n// round to be giving one nanosecond of CPU to every non-empty queue\n// in the apiserver (where emptiness is judged based on both queued\n// and executing requests from that queue), and define R(t) = (server\n// start time) + (1 ns) * (number of rounds since server start). Let\n// us write NEQ(t) for that number of non-empty queues in the\n// apiserver at time t.  Let us also write C for the concurrency\n// limit.  In the original paper, the partial derivative of R(t) with\n// respect to t is\n//\n//\t1 / NEQ(t) .\n//\n// To generalize from transmitting one packet at a time to executing C\n// requests at a time, that derivative becomes\n//\n//\tC / NEQ(t) .\n//\n// However, sometimes there are fewer than C requests available to\n// execute.  For a given queue \"q\", let us also write \"reqs(q, t)\" for\n// the number of requests of that queue that are executing at that\n// time.  The total number of requests executing is sum[over q]\n// reqs(q, t) and if that is less than C then virtual time is not\n// advancing as fast as it would if all C seats were occupied; in this\n// case the numerator of the quotient in that derivative should be\n// adjusted proportionally.  Putting it all together for fair queing\n// for server requests: at a particular time t, the partial derivative\n// of R(t) with respect to t is\n//\n//\tmin( C, sum[over q] reqs(q, t) ) / NEQ(t) .\n//\n// In terms of the implementation outline, this is the rate at which\n// virtual time is advancing at time t (in virtual nanoseconds per\n// real nanosecond). Where the networking implementation outline adds\n// packet size to a virtual time, in our version this corresponds to\n// adding a service time (i.e., duration) to virtual time.\n//\n// The third difference is handled by modifying the algorithm to\n// dispatch based on an initial guess at the request's service time\n// (duration) and then make the corresponding adjustments once the\n// request's actual service time is known. This is similar, although\n// not exactly isomorphic, to the original paper's adjustment by\n// `$\\delta$` for the sake of promptness.\n//\n// For implementation simplicity (see below), let us use the same\n// initial service time guess for every request; call that duration\n// G. A good choice might be the service time limit (1\n// minute). Different guesses will give slightly different dynamics,\n// but any positive number can be used for G without ruining the\n// long-term behavior.\n//\n// As in ordinary fair queuing, there is a bound on divergence from\n// the ideal. In plain fair queuing the bound is one packet; in our\n// version it is C requests.\n//\n// To support efficiently making the necessary adjustments once a\n// request's actual service time is known, the virtual finish time of\n// a request and the last virtual finish time of a queue are not\n// represented directly but instead computed from queue length,\n// request position in the queue, and an alternate state variable that\n// holds the queue's virtual start time. While the queue is empty and\n// has no requests executing: the value of its virtual start time\n// variable is ignored and its last virtual finish time is considered\n// to be in the virtual past. When a request arrives to an empty queue\n// with no requests executing, the queue's virtual start time is set\n// to the current virtual time. The virtual finish time of request\n// number J in the queue (counting from J=1 for the head) is J * G +\n// (queue's virtual start time). While the queue is non-empty: the\n// last virtual finish time of the queue is the virtual finish time of\n// the last request in the queue. While the queue is empty and has a\n// request executing: the last virtual finish time is the queue's\n// virtual start time. When a request is dequeued for service the\n// queue's virtual start time is advanced by G. When a request\n// finishes being served, and the actual service time was S, the\n// queue's virtual start time is decremented by G - S.\npackage queueset\n","numLines":120,"startLine":1,"totalLines":120}}}
